If overload resolution chooses a generic method as the one to\\n28: invoke then the inferred type arguments are used as the actual type arguments for the\\n29: invocation. The failure of type inference, in and of itself,\\n31: does not cause a compile-time error. However, it often leads to a compile-time error\\n32: when overload resolution then fails to find any applicable methods.\\n33: \\n34: If the supplied number of arguments is different than the number of parameters in\\n35: the method, then inference immediately fails. {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01FM4kFh4XKVbQAc4pPmvCg5","type":"tool_result","content":"{\"content\":\"200:             // call site, not the declared types.\\n201:             //\\n202:             // Contrast that with this scenario:\\n203:             //\\n204:             // Scenario Two:\\n205:             //\\n206:             // interface I<T> \\n207:             // { \\n208:             //    void M<U>(T t, U u); \\n209:             // }\\n210:             // ...\\n211:             // static void Goo<V>(V v, I<V> iv) \\n212:             // {\\n213:             //   iv.M(v, \\\"\\\");\\n214:             // }\\n215:             //\\n216:             // Obviously inference should succeed here; it should infer that U is string. \\n217:             //\\n218:             // But consider what happens during the inference process on the first argument.\\n219:             // The first thing we will do is say \\\"what's the type of the argument? V. What's\\n220:             // the type of the corresponding formal parameter? The first formal parameter of\\n221:             // I<V>.M<whatever> is of type V. The inference engine will then say \\\"V is a \\n222:             // method type parameter, and therefore we have an inference from V to V\\\". \\n223:             // But *V* is not one of the method type parameters being inferred; the only \\n224:             // method type parameter being inferred here is *U*.\\n225:             //\\n226:             // This is perhaps some evidence that the formal parameters passed in should be\\n227:             // the formal parameters of the *declared* method; in this case, (T, U), not\\n228:             // the formal parameters of the *constructed* method, (V, U). \\n229:             //\\n230:             // However, one might make the argument that no, we could just add a check\\n231:             // to ensure that if we see a method type parameter as a formal parameter type,\\n232:             // then we only perform the inference if the method type parameter is a type \\n233:             // parameter of the method for which inference is being performed.\\n234:             //\\n235:             // Unfortunately, that does not work either:\\n236:             //\\n237:             // Scenario three:\\n238:             //\\n239:             // class C<T>\\n240:             // {\\n241:             //   static void M<U>(T t, U u)\\n242:             //   {\\n243:             //     ...\\n244:             //     C<U>.M(u, 123);\\n245:             //     ...\\n246:             //   }\\n247:             // }\\n248:             //\\n249:             // The *original* formal parameter types are (T, U); the *constructed* formal parameter types\\n250:             // are (U, U), but *those are logically two different U's*. The first U is from the outer caller;\\n251:             // the second U is the U of the recursive call.\\n252:             //\\n253:             // That is, suppose someone called C<string>.M<double>(string, double).  The recursive call should be to\\n254:             // C<double>.M<int>(double, int). We should absolutely not make an inference on the first argument\\n255:             // from U to U just because C<U>.M<something>'s first formal parameter is of type U.  If we did then\\n256:             // inference would fail, because we'd end up with two bounds on 'U' -- 'U' and 'int'. First phase: Makes initial inferences of bounds on unfixed type variables\\n2. Second phase: Fixes type parameters to specific types and infers further bounds\\n\\nThis struct maintains extensive state for tracking fixed/unfixed type parameters and their bounds (exact, upper, lower) as specified in the C# spec.\\n\\n### 3. Method Comparison (OverloadResolution.cs - BetterFunctionMember)\\nOnce candidates are deemed applicable, the **BetterFunctionMember** method (lines 1951 and 1990) compares pairs of applicable members.
